[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is mostly my wife. She’s been after me for a while, saying I’m not eating enough, and she thought I ought to talk to someone about it. I don’t know that I’d have come in on my own, to be honest.\n\nMost days I just have coffee and a piece of toast in the morning, and then later on I might have some soup, or maybe some vegetables, something light like that. I don’t really see the need for big meals anymore. At my age, they just feel unnecessary, and sitting down to a full plate seems like more than I need. My wife worries about it, but to me it just feels… sensible, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight with this eating pattern?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way, like I’m on some sort of diet. But… yes, I suppose part of it is that I don’t want to put weight back on.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding foolish. If my wife puts butter on my toast, or she makes up a full plate for me at supper, I get this uneasy feeling, like if I start eating that way again I’ll lose the control I’ve managed to keep. I know she thinks she’s just helping, and maybe she is, but to me it feels like too much. I start thinking, “Well, if I have that today, then tomorrow it’ll be more,” and before I know it I’ll be gaining weight again.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m trying to lose a lot of weight, exactly. It’s more that I’m trying not to go back the other way. Keeping things light feels safer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: Underweight? I don’t really think of myself that way, no. I mean, I know I’m thinner than I used to be, and my wife keeps saying it like it’s some urgent matter, but I don’t quite see why everyone is making such a fuss over it.\n\nAt my age, people don’t eat the way they did when they were younger, do they? You slow down, you don’t need great big meals, and it seems natural to me that your body changes a bit. I’m still getting around the house, still doing my chores, still managing to go on my little tours and outings when I want to. So from my point of view, if I’m still functioning and doing what I need to do, it’s hard for me to see it as being dangerously underweight or anything like that.\n\nMaybe the numbers look low to other people, I don’t know, but I don’t feel like I’m wasting away the way they seem to imply. I feel more like I’ve just adjusted to needing less.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Afraid is a strong word, but… yes, I suppose I am, in my own way. It’s not like I sit there trembling over a pound or two, but when my wife tries to put butter on my toast, or she serves me a proper full plate, I get very uneasy.\n\nI start thinking that if I let that happen, if I go back to eating like that, I’ll start putting weight back on. And then it feels like all the control I’ve managed to keep will slip away. I know she means well, and I know to her it probably just looks like a normal meal, but to me it feels like the beginning of losing hold of things.\n\nSo yes… I do get nervous about gaining weight. Maybe more than I like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose it depends what you mean by significant. My doctor does say the number on the scale has got too low, and he seemed rather concerned about it. He looks at it very differently than I do, I think.\n\nTo me, being lighter hasn’t felt entirely like a bad thing. I can get around the garden a bit more easily, and when I go for my walks I feel like I’m not carrying as much of myself, if that makes sense. I know that may sound like I’m brushing it off, and perhaps I am a little, but that’s honestly how it feels to me day to day.\n\nSo yes, the number has gone down enough that my doctor is saying it’s too low. I just don’t always feel it in the alarming way other people seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would stop restricting your food intake if you reached a weight that you consider acceptable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there is some particular number where I’d suddenly say, “Right, that’s acceptable, now I’ll eat normally again.” It doesn’t really work that neatly in my head.\n\nI suppose what brings me in, more than anything, is that my wife thinks I’m not eating enough. She watches what I have and gets upset about it. But to me, most days, coffee and a piece of toast in the morning feels perfectly adequate. Then later on I might have a bit of soup, or some vegetables, something light. I don’t feel the need for a full plate the way I used to.\n\nAt my age, big meals just feel unnecessary, almost excessive. So if I reached some weight other people considered better, I’m not sure I’d suddenly want to start eating more. I’d probably still feel that smaller meals make sense for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently lower than it should be?\n\nPatient: Lower than it should be… well, I suppose that’s where I struggle a bit, because I don’t really see it the way everyone else seems to.\n\nMy wife and the doctor look at me and act as though it’s this very serious thing, and I don’t mean to dismiss them, but I honestly don’t understand why there’s quite so much fuss about it. I’m 78, you know. People my age aren’t meant to eat like they did at 40 or 50. You slow down, you don’t need as much, your appetite isn’t the same, and carrying less weight doesn’t strike me as automatically bad.\n\nI’m still getting through my usual bits and pieces. I do my chores around the house, I keep up with things in the garden, I go out for my walks and little tours around where I like to go. If I were lying in bed all day or unable to manage, maybe I’d think differently. But as long as I’m still doing those things, it’s hard for me to feel that my weight is dangerously low or anything like that.\n\nSo… lower than some people would like, yes, clearly. Lower than it “should” be? I’m not fully convinced of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner is more attractive or desirable?\n\nPatient: Attractive… I don’t know that I’d use that word exactly, not at my age. It’s not as though I’m trying to look handsome or impress anyone, really. But desirable, perhaps, in the sense that I feel more… proper, maybe, when there’s less of me.\n\nI notice little things. If I put my belt on and it sits a notch looser, there’s a part of me that thinks, well, good, I’ve done something right. Like I’ve kept myself in hand. It feels tidy somehow, controlled. I know that may sound odd, but it gives me a sort of satisfaction.\n\nAnd then other days, if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little rounded, I feel quite put off. Sloppy is the word that comes to mind. Disappointed in myself, even if no one else would notice it. I’ll stand there and think, no, that doesn’t look right, that’s not how I want to be.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m chasing being “attractive,” exactly. But thinner does feel better to me. It feels more acceptable. More like I’m doing things properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control over your eating if you started having bigger meals again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that is what worries me, though I don’t always put it in those words.\n\nIf my wife serves me what she calls a “proper” meal, a full plate, I feel myself tense up before I’ve even started. Or if she tries to put butter on toast, or says, “You need a bit more than that,” I get nervous. It’s not just the food itself, it’s the feeling that if I give in to that, then I’ll start going back the other way — putting weight on, losing the control I’ve worked quite hard to keep.\n\nI know one meal probably doesn’t sound like much to someone else. But to me it feels like the beginning of something. Like if I start having bigger portions again, then where does it stop? I’ve kept things quite measured for myself, and when that gets disrupted, I feel unsettled. So yes, I suppose there is a fear there that I’d lose my grip on it.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to maintain your current weight or to continue losing weight?\n\nPatient: I’d say my goal is mostly to stay where I am… though if I’m honest, I do get anxious about going upward. That’s the part I notice more than any wish to keep losing and losing.\n\nWhen my wife puts a full plate in front of me, or tries to add butter to something, I feel this sort of tightness straightaway, like, no, that’s too much. It isn’t just the meal in front of me. It’s what I imagine comes after it — that I’ll start putting weight back on, and then I’ll lose the control I feel I’ve worked quite hard to keep.\n\nSo I tell myself I’m trying to maintain things, keep it steady, keep it sensible. But really, I suppose I’m also trying very hard not to gain. That’s what makes me nervous. If I eat more than I’m comfortable with, it feels like the beginning of slipping backwards.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happy with your body if you were at a healthy weight for your age and height?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I would be, no. I know “healthy weight” sounds sensible, and I don’t mean to argue with the idea of it, but when I picture it on myself, I don’t feel comfortable.\n\nIt’s the small things that seem to affect me more than they probably should. If my belt feels a notch looser, I get this quiet feeling that I’ve done right by myself somehow, like I’ve kept myself in order. It reassures me. But if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit rounded, I feel… disappointed, really. Sloppy is the word that comes to mind, though I know that may sound harsh.\n\nSo if being at a healthy weight meant seeing more of that roundness, or feeling my clothes tighter, I don’t think I’d feel happy with it. I might understand it in my head, but emotionally, no, I think I’d struggle.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to eat as little as you do now if you didn't have to worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose if gaining weight truly wasn’t a worry at all, then maybe I wouldn’t feel the need to keep it quite so tight… but it’s hard for me to imagine that, because the worry is sort of wrapped up in the eating.\n\nWhen my wife tries to put butter on things, or gives me what she thinks is a decent full plate, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s a bit more food.” I immediately get nervous that it’s the start of putting weight back on. It feels like if I allow that, then I’m giving up the control I’ve worked quite hard to keep. So the eating less is partly because I’m afraid of what happens if I don’t.\n\nIf you could somehow take away that fear completely, then maybe I wouldn’t be so strict. But sitting here honestly, I can’t really separate the two in my mind. The thought of eating more brings the thought of weight coming back almost straight after it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still restrict your food intake if you knew you couldn't gain weight no matter what you ate?\n\nPatient: If I truly knew I couldn’t gain weight no matter what I ate… I suppose I might not feel the same need to restrict it, at least not in the same way. But it’s difficult, because in my mind food and weight are so tied together that I don’t really know how to separate them.\n\nWhen my wife puts a full plate in front of me, or if she tries to add butter to something, I feel nervous almost immediately. It’s not just, “That’s a bit rich,” or “I’m not hungry.” It’s more that I picture myself starting to put weight back on, and then I feel like I’d lose the control I’ve worked hard to keep. That thought comes so quickly that it’s hard to imagine eating freely, even if someone told me there would be no weight gain.\n\nSo… maybe in theory I wouldn’t restrict as much. But emotionally, I think I’d still be cautious, because the fear is right there before I’ve even had a chance to think it through.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern about eating more that you will gain weight, or that you will not be as thin as you want to be?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight, yes… though the two feel very close together to me. It’s not like I sit there and think in a neat way, “I want to be this thin.” It’s more that when eating more is put in front of me, I immediately worry it will lead to weight coming back.\n\nLike when my wife tries to put butter on something, or she gives me what she considers a proper full plate, I can feel myself tense up before I’ve even eaten it. I start thinking, “If I let this happen, this is how it starts,” and then I’ll be putting weight back on and losing the control I’ve tried so hard to keep. That’s the part that frightens me.\n\nSo I suppose not being as thin as I’d like is part of it, but the main thing is the weight gain — the sense that eating more means things will get away from me.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating mostly because you are afraid of what will happen if you gain weight, or is it because you want to maintain a certain body image?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear of what happens if I gain weight, I think. The body image part is there, but it isn’t as clear in my head as, “I must look exactly like this.” It’s more that if I eat more, I immediately imagine the weight coming back, and then I feel like I’ve lost control.\n\nLike I’ve said, when my wife tries to put butter on something, or she gives me a plate that she thinks is just a normal full meal, I get nervous straightaway. I can feel myself thinking, “This is how it starts,” as if one bit more will lead to another, and then suddenly I’ll be putting weight back on. It’s not really calm or logical in the moment. It feels like I’ve worked to keep things under control, and eating more threatens that.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight and losing that control, rather than just wanting to maintain a particular image.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or about the health problems it could cause?\n\nPatient: It’s more about how I think I would look, I suppose… or maybe not just “look,” but what it would mean if I saw the weight coming back on me. I don’t really sit there thinking about health problems from gaining weight. That’s not what usually comes into my head.\n\nWhat happens is more immediate than that. If my wife puts a full plate in front of me, or she says, “You need a bit of butter on that,” I get this nervous feeling straightaway. I start thinking that if I allow that, then I’ll start putting weight back on, and then I’ll lose the control I’ve been trying so hard to keep. It feels like one thing will lead to another.\n\nSo no, it’s not really that I’m frightened of health problems from gaining weight. It’s more the thought of my body changing and me not being able to stop it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to restrict your food intake if being thin wasn't a consideration for you?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, probably not in the same way. If being thin truly didn’t matter to me at all, I don’t know that I’d be so fixed on cutting back.\n\nBecause a lot of it is tied up in those little signs I look for, even if I don’t always say that out loud. Like if my belt feels a notch looser, there’s this feeling of, well, I’ve done right by myself today. As if that means I’ve kept myself in order. But then if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the slightest bit rounded, I feel sloppy… disappointed in myself, really. It can change how I feel about the whole day.\n\nSo when you ask if I’d still restrict if thinness wasn’t part of it, I suppose the answer is that thinness is very much part of it. Not always in some grand way, but in those small checks and feelings. The belt, the mirror, how my middle looks. Those things do affect whether I feel I should eat less.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I don’t feel the need for a full plate the way I used to.\n\nAt my age, big meals just feel unnecessary, almost excessive. So if I reached some weight other people considered better, I’m not sure I’d suddenly want to start eating more. I’d probably still feel that smaller meals make sense for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently lower than it should be?\n\nPatient: Lower than it should be… well, I suppose that’s where I struggle a bit, because I don’t really see it the way everyone else seems to.\n\nMy wife and the doctor look at me and act as though it’s this very serious thing, and I don’t mean to dismiss them, but I honestly don’t understand why there’s quite so much fuss about it. I’m 78, you know. People my age aren’t meant to eat like they did at 40 or 50. You slow down, you don’t need as much, your appetite isn’t the same, and carrying less weight doesn’t strike me as automatically bad.\n\nI’m still getting through my usual bits and pieces. I do my chores around the house, I keep up with things in the garden, I go out for my walks and little tours around where I like to go. If I were lying in bed all day or unable to manage, maybe I’d think differently. But as long as I’m still doing those things, it’s hard for me to feel that my weight is dangerously low or anything like that.\n\nSo… lower than some people would like, yes, clearly. Lower than it “should” be? I’m not fully convinced of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner is more attractive or desirable?\n\nPatient: Attractive… I don’t know that I’d use that word exactly, not at my age. It’s not as though I’m trying to look handsome or impress anyone, really. But desirable, perhaps, in the sense that I feel more… proper, maybe, when there’s less of me.\n\nI notice little things. If I put my belt on and it sits a notch looser, there’s a part of me that thinks, well, good, I’ve done something right. Like I’ve kept myself in hand. It feels tidy somehow, controlled. I know that may sound odd, but it gives me a sort of satisfaction.\n\nAnd then other days, if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little rounded, I feel quite put off. Sloppy is the word that comes to mind. Disappointed in myself, even if no one else would notice it. I’ll stand there and think, no, that doesn’t look right, that’s not how I want to be.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m chasing being “attractive,” exactly. But thinner does feel better to me. It feels more acceptable. More like I’m doing things properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control over your eating if you started having bigger meals again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that is what worries me, though I don’t always put it in those words.\n\nIf my wife serves me what she calls a “proper” meal, a full plate, I feel myself tense up before I’ve even started. Or if she tries to put butter on toast, or says, “You need a bit more than that,” I get nervous. It’s not just the food itself, it’s the feeling that if I give in to that, then I’ll start going back the other way — putting weight on, losing the control I’ve worked quite hard to keep.\n\nI know one meal probably doesn’t sound like much to someone else. But to me it feels like the beginning of something. Like if I start having bigger portions again, then where does it stop? I’ve kept things quite measured for myself, and when that gets disrupted, I feel unsettled. So yes, I suppose there is a fear there that I’d lose my grip on it.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to maintain your current weight or to continue losing weight?\n\nPatient: I’d say my goal is mostly to stay where I am… though if I’m honest, I do get anxious about going upward. That’s the part I notice more than any wish to keep losing and losing.\n\nWhen my wife puts a full plate in front of me, or tries to add butter to something, I feel this sort of tightness straightaway, like, no, that’s too much. It isn’t just the meal in front of me. It’s what I imagine comes after it — that I’ll start putting weight back on, and then I’ll lose the control I feel I’ve worked quite hard to keep.\n\nSo I tell myself I’m trying to maintain things, keep it steady, keep it sensible. But really, I suppose I’m also trying very hard not to gain. That’s what makes me nervous. If I eat more than I’m comfortable with, it feels like the beginning of slipping backwards.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happy with your body if you were at a healthy weight for your age and height?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I would be, no. I know “healthy weight” sounds sensible, and I don’t mean to argue with the idea of it, but when I picture it on myself, I don’t feel comfortable.\n\nIt’s the small things that seem to affect me more than they probably should. If my belt feels a notch looser, I get this quiet feeling that I’ve done right by myself somehow, like I’ve kept myself in order. It reassures me. But if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit rounded, I feel… disappointed, really. Sloppy is the word that comes to mind, though I know that may sound harsh.\n\nSo if being at a healthy weight meant seeing more of that roundness, or feeling my clothes tighter, I don’t think I’d feel happy with it. I might understand it in my head, but emotionally, no, I think I’d struggle.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to eat as little as you do now if you didn't have to worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose if gaining weight truly wasn’t a worry at all, then maybe I wouldn’t feel the need to keep it quite so tight… but it’s hard for me to imagine that, because the worry is sort of wrapped up in the eating.\n\nWhen my wife tries to put butter on things, or gives me what she thinks is a decent full plate, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s a bit more food.” I immediately get nervous that it’s the start of putting weight back on. It feels like if I allow that, then I’m giving up the control I’ve worked quite hard to keep. So the eating less is partly because I’m afraid of what happens if I don’t.\n\nIf you could somehow take away that fear completely, then maybe I wouldn’t be so strict. But sitting here honestly, I can’t really separate the two in my mind. The thought of eating more brings the thought of weight coming back almost straight after it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still restrict your food intake if you knew you couldn't gain weight no matter what you ate?\n\nPatient: If I truly knew I couldn’t gain weight no matter what I ate… I suppose I might not feel the same need to restrict it, at least not in the same way. But it’s difficult, because in my mind food and weight are so tied together that I don’t really know how to separate them.\n\nWhen my wife puts a full plate in front of me, or if she tries to add butter to something, I feel nervous almost immediately. It’s not just, “That’s a bit rich,” or “I’m not hungry.” It’s more that I picture myself starting to put weight back on, and then I feel like I’d lose the control I’ve worked hard to keep. That thought comes so quickly that it’s hard to imagine eating freely, even if someone told me there would be no weight gain.\n\nSo… maybe in theory I wouldn’t restrict as much. But emotionally, I think I’d still be cautious, because the fear is right there before I’ve even had a chance to think it through.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern about eating more that you will gain weight, or that you will not be as thin as you want to be?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight, yes… though the two feel very close together to me. It’s not like I sit there and think in a neat way, “I want to be this thin.” It’s more that when eating more is put in front of me, I immediately worry it will lead to weight coming back.\n\nLike when my wife tries to put butter on something, or she gives me what she considers a proper full plate, I can feel myself tense up before I’ve even eaten it. I start thinking, “If I let this happen, this is how it starts,” and then I’ll be putting weight back on and losing the control I’ve tried so hard to keep. That’s the part that frightens me.\n\nSo I suppose not being as thin as I’d like is part of it, but the main thing is the weight gain — the sense that eating more means things will get away from me.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating mostly because you are afraid of what will happen if you gain weight, or is it because you want to maintain a certain body image?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear of what happens if I gain weight, I think. The body image part is there, but it isn’t as clear in my head as, “I must look exactly like this.” It’s more that if I eat more, I immediately imagine the weight coming back, and then I feel like I’ve lost control.\n\nLike I’ve said, when my wife tries to put butter on something, or she gives me a plate that she thinks is just a normal full meal, I get nervous straightaway. I can feel myself thinking, “This is how it starts,” as if one bit more will lead to another, and then suddenly I’ll be putting weight back on. It’s not really calm or logical in the moment. It feels like I’ve worked to keep things under control, and eating more threatens that.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight and losing that control, rather than just wanting to maintain a particular image.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or about the health problems it could cause?\n\nPatient: It’s more about how I think I would look, I suppose… or maybe not just “look,” but what it would mean if I saw the weight coming back on me. I don’t really sit there thinking about health problems from gaining weight. That’s not what usually comes into my head.\n\nWhat happens is more immediate than that. If my wife puts a full plate in front of me, or she says, “You need a bit of butter on that,” I get this nervous feeling straightaway. I start thinking that if I allow that, then I’ll start putting weight back on, and then I’ll lose the control I’ve been trying so hard to keep. It feels like one thing will lead to another.\n\nSo no, it’s not really that I’m frightened of health problems from gaining weight. It’s more the thought of my body changing and me not being able to stop it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to restrict your food intake if being thin wasn't a consideration for you?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, probably not in the same way. If being thin truly didn’t matter to me at all, I don’t know that I’d be so fixed on cutting back.\n\nBecause a lot of it is tied up in those little signs I look for, even if I don’t always say that out loud. Like if my belt feels a notch looser, there’s this feeling of, well, I’ve done right by myself today. As if that means I’ve kept myself in order. But then if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the slightest bit rounded, I feel sloppy… disappointed in myself, really. It can change how I feel about the whole day.\n\nSo when you ask if I’d still restrict if thinness wasn’t part of it, I suppose the answer is that thinness is very much part of it. Not always in some grand way, but in those small checks and feelings. The belt, the mirror, how my middle looks. Those things do affect whether I feel I should eat less.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mostly about avoiding weight gain, I think. The shape part is there somewhere, but the thing that really grabs me in the moment is the fear that if I eat more, the weight will start coming back.

Like when my wife puts butter on something, or she gives me what she sees as a proper full plate, I can feel myself tense up before I’ve even started eating. I start thinking, “If I let this happen, that’s the beginning of it.” Not just that one meal, but the next one and the next, and then I won’t be able to stop it. It feels like I’ll lose the control I’ve worked hard to keep.

So yes, I’d say it’s less, “I’m trying to get to one exact body shape,” and more, “I’m frightened of putting weight back on.” That’s what makes me want to eat less.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is mostly my wife. She’s been after me for a while, saying I’m not eating enough, and she thought I ought to talk to someone about it. I don’t know that I’d have come in on my own, to be honest.\n\nMost days I just have coffee and a piece of toast in the morning, and then later on I might have some soup, or maybe some vegetables, something light like that. I don’t really see the need for big meals anymore. At my age, they just feel unnecessary, and sitting down to a full plate seems like more than I need. My wife worries about it, but to me it just feels… sensible, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight with this eating pattern?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way, like I’m on some sort of diet. But… yes, I suppose part of it is that I don’t want to put weight back on.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding foolish. If my wife puts butter on my toast, or she makes up a full plate for me at supper, I get this uneasy feeling, like if I start eating that way again I’ll lose the control I’ve managed to keep. I know she thinks she’s just helping, and maybe she is, but to me it feels like too much. I start thinking, “Well, if I have that today, then tomorrow it’ll be more,” and before I know it I’ll be gaining weight again.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m trying to lose a lot of weight, exactly. It’s more that I’m trying not to go back the other way. Keeping things light feels safer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: Underweight? I don’t really think of myself that way, no. I mean, I know I’m thinner than I used to be, and my wife keeps saying it like it’s some urgent matter, but I don’t quite see why everyone is making such a fuss over it.\n\nAt my age, people don’t eat the way they did when they were younger, do they? You slow down, you don’t need great big meals, and it seems natural to me that your body changes a bit. I’m still getting around the house, still doing my chores, still managing to go on my little tours and outings when I want to. So from my point of view, if I’m still functioning and doing what I need to do, it’s hard for me to see it as being dangerously underweight or anything like that.\n\nMaybe the numbers look low to other people, I don’t know, but I don’t feel like I’m wasting away the way they seem to imply. I feel more like I’ve just adjusted to needing less.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Afraid is a strong word, but… yes, I suppose I am, in my own way. It’s not like I sit there trembling over a pound or two, but when my wife tries to put butter on my toast, or she serves me a proper full plate, I get very uneasy.\n\nI start thinking that if I let that happen, if I go back to eating like that, I’ll start putting weight back on. And then it feels like all the control I’ve managed to keep will slip away. I know she means well, and I know to her it probably just looks like a normal meal, but to me it feels like the beginning of losing hold of things.\n\nSo yes… I do get nervous about gaining weight. Maybe more than I like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose it depends what you mean by significant. My doctor does say the number on the scale has got too low, and he seemed rather concerned about it. He looks at it very differently than I do, I think.\n\nTo me, being lighter hasn’t felt entirely like a bad thing. I can get around the garden a bit more easily, and when I go for my walks I feel like I’m not carrying as much of myself, if that makes sense. I know that may sound like I’m brushing it off, and perhaps I am a little, but that’s honestly how it feels to me day to day.\n\nSo yes, the number has gone down enough that my doctor is saying it’s too low. I just don’t always feel it in the alarming way other people seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would stop restricting your food intake if you reached a weight that you consider acceptable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there is some particular number where I’d suddenly say, “Right, that’s acceptable, now I’ll eat normally again.” It doesn’t really work that neatly in my head.\n\nI suppose what brings me in, more than anything, is that my wife thinks I’m not eating enough. She watches what I have and gets upset about it. But to me, most days, coffee and a piece of toast in the morning feels perfectly adequate. Then later on I might have a bit of soup, or some vegetables, something light. I don’t feel the need for a full plate the way I used to.\n\nAt my age, big meals just feel unnecessary, almost excessive. So if I reached some weight other people considered better, I’m not sure I’d suddenly want to start eating more. I’d probably still feel that smaller meals make sense for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently lower than it should be?\n\nPatient: Lower than it should be… well, I suppose that’s where I struggle a bit, because I don’t really see it the way everyone else seems to.\n\nMy wife and the doctor look at me and act as though it’s this very serious thing, and I don’t mean to dismiss them, but I honestly don’t understand why there’s quite so much fuss about it. I’m 78, you know. People my age aren’t meant to eat like they did at 40 or 50. You slow down, you don’t need as much, your appetite isn’t the same, and carrying less weight doesn’t strike me as automatically bad.\n\nI’m still getting through my usual bits and pieces. I do my chores around the house, I keep up with things in the garden, I go out for my walks and little tours around where I like to go. If I were lying in bed all day or unable to manage, maybe I’d think differently. But as long as I’m still doing those things, it’s hard for me to feel that my weight is dangerously low or anything like that.\n\nSo… lower than some people would like, yes, clearly. Lower than it “should” be? I’m not fully convinced of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner is more attractive or desirable?\n\nPatient: Attractive… I don’t know that I’d use that word exactly, not at my age. It’s not as though I’m trying to look handsome or impress anyone, really. But desirable, perhaps, in the sense that I feel more… proper, maybe, when there’s less of me.\n\nI notice little things. If I put my belt on and it sits a notch looser, there’s a part of me that thinks, well, good, I’ve done something right. Like I’ve kept myself in hand. It feels tidy somehow, controlled. I know that may sound odd, but it gives me a sort of satisfaction.\n\nAnd then other days, if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little rounded, I feel quite put off. Sloppy is the word that comes to mind. Disappointed in myself, even if no one else would notice it. I’ll stand there and think, no, that doesn’t look right, that’s not how I want to be.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m chasing being “attractive,” exactly. But thinner does feel better to me. It feels more acceptable. More like I’m doing things properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control over your eating if you started having bigger meals again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that is what worries me, though I don’t always put it in those words.\n\nIf my wife serves me what she calls a “proper” meal, a full plate, I feel myself tense up before I’ve even started. Or if she tries to put butter on toast, or says, “You need a bit more than that,” I get nervous. It’s not just the food itself, it’s the feeling that if I give in to that, then I’ll start going back the other way — putting weight on, losing the control I’ve worked quite hard to keep.\n\nI know one meal probably doesn’t sound like much to someone else. But to me it feels like the beginning of something. Like if I start having bigger portions again, then where does it stop? I’ve kept things quite measured for myself, and when that gets disrupted, I feel unsettled. So yes, I suppose there is a fear there that I’d lose my grip on it.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to maintain your current weight or to continue losing weight?\n\nPatient: I’d say my goal is mostly to stay where I am… though if I’m honest, I do get anxious about going upward. That’s the part I notice more than any wish to keep losing and losing.\n\nWhen my wife puts a full plate in front of me, or tries to add butter to something, I feel this sort of tightness straightaway, like, no, that’s too much. It isn’t just the meal in front of me. It’s what I imagine comes after it — that I’ll start putting weight back on, and then I’ll lose the control I feel I’ve worked quite hard to keep.\n\nSo I tell myself I’m trying to maintain things, keep it steady, keep it sensible. But really, I suppose I’m also trying very hard not to gain. That’s what makes me nervous. If I eat more than I’m comfortable with, it feels like the beginning of slipping backwards.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happy with your body if you were at a healthy weight for your age and height?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I would be, no. I know “healthy weight” sounds sensible, and I don’t mean to argue with the idea of it, but when I picture it on myself, I don’t feel comfortable.\n\nIt’s the small things that seem to affect me more than they probably should. If my belt feels a notch looser, I get this quiet feeling that I’ve done right by myself somehow, like I’ve kept myself in order. It reassures me. But if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit rounded, I feel… disappointed, really. Sloppy is the word that comes to mind, though I know that may sound harsh.\n\nSo if being at a healthy weight meant seeing more of that roundness, or feeling my clothes tighter, I don’t think I’d feel happy with it. I might understand it in my head, but emotionally, no, I think I’d struggle.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to eat as little as you do now if you didn't have to worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose if gaining weight truly wasn’t a worry at all, then maybe I wouldn’t feel the need to keep it quite so tight… but it’s hard for me to imagine that, because the worry is sort of wrapped up in the eating.\n\nWhen my wife tries to put butter on things, or gives me what she thinks is a decent full plate, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s a bit more food.” I immediately get nervous that it’s the start of putting weight back on. It feels like if I allow that, then I’m giving up the control I’ve worked quite hard to keep. So the eating less is partly because I’m afraid of what happens if I don’t.\n\nIf you could somehow take away that fear completely, then maybe I wouldn’t be so strict. But sitting here honestly, I can’t really separate the two in my mind. The thought of eating more brings the thought of weight coming back almost straight after it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still restrict your food intake if you knew you couldn't gain weight no matter what you ate?\n\nPatient: If I truly knew I couldn’t gain weight no matter what I ate… I suppose I might not feel the same need to restrict it, at least not in the same way. But it’s difficult, because in my mind food and weight are so tied together that I don’t really know how to separate them.\n\nWhen my wife puts a full plate in front of me, or if she tries to add butter to something, I feel nervous almost immediately. It’s not just, “That’s a bit rich,” or “I’m not hungry.” It’s more that I picture myself starting to put weight back on, and then I feel like I’d lose the control I’ve worked hard to keep. That thought comes so quickly that it’s hard to imagine eating freely, even if someone told me there would be no weight gain.\n\nSo… maybe in theory I wouldn’t restrict as much. But emotionally, I think I’d still be cautious, because the fear is right there before I’ve even had a chance to think it through.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern about eating more that you will gain weight, or that you will not be as thin as you want to be?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight, yes… though the two feel very close together to me. It’s not like I sit there and think in a neat way, “I want to be this thin.” It’s more that when eating more is put in front of me, I immediately worry it will lead to weight coming back.\n\nLike when my wife tries to put butter on something, or she gives me what she considers a proper full plate, I can feel myself tense up before I’ve even eaten it. I start thinking, “If I let this happen, this is how it starts,” and then I’ll be putting weight back on and losing the control I’ve tried so hard to keep. That’s the part that frightens me.\n\nSo I suppose not being as thin as I’d like is part of it, but the main thing is the weight gain — the sense that eating more means things will get away from me.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating mostly because you are afraid of what will happen if you gain weight, or is it because you want to maintain a certain body image?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear of what happens if I gain weight, I think. The body image part is there, but it isn’t as clear in my head as, “I must look exactly like this.” It’s more that if I eat more, I immediately imagine the weight coming back, and then I feel like I’ve lost control.\n\nLike I’ve said, when my wife tries to put butter on something, or she gives me a plate that she thinks is just a normal full meal, I get nervous straightaway. I can feel myself thinking, “This is how it starts,” as if one bit more will lead to another, and then suddenly I’ll be putting weight back on. It’s not really calm or logical in the moment. It feels like I’ve worked to keep things under control, and eating more threatens that.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight and losing that control, rather than just wanting to maintain a particular image.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or about the health problems it could cause?\n\nPatient: It’s more about how I think I would look, I suppose… or maybe not just “look,” but what it would mean if I saw the weight coming back on me. I don’t really sit there thinking about health problems from gaining weight. That’s not what usually comes into my head.\n\nWhat happens is more immediate than that. If my wife puts a full plate in front of me, or she says, “You need a bit of butter on that,” I get this nervous feeling straightaway. I start thinking that if I allow that, then I’ll start putting weight back on, and then I’ll lose the control I’ve been trying so hard to keep. It feels like one thing will lead to another.\n\nSo no, it’s not really that I’m frightened of health problems from gaining weight. It’s more the thought of my body changing and me not being able to stop it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to restrict your food intake if being thin wasn't a consideration for you?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, probably not in the same way. If being thin truly didn’t matter to me at all, I don’t know that I’d be so fixed on cutting back.\n\nBecause a lot of it is tied up in those little signs I look for, even if I don’t always say that out loud. Like if my belt feels a notch looser, there’s this feeling of, well, I’ve done right by myself today. As if that means I’ve kept myself in order. But then if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the slightest bit rounded, I feel sloppy… disappointed in myself, really. It can change how I feel about the whole day.\n\nSo when you ask if I’d still restrict if thinness wasn’t part of it, I suppose the answer is that thinness is very much part of it. Not always in some grand way, but in those small checks and feelings. The belt, the mirror, how my middle looks. Those things do affect whether I feel I should eat less.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to eat less mostly about achieving a certain body shape or mostly about avoiding weight gain?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about avoiding weight gain, I think. The shape part is there somewhere, but the thing that really grabs me in the moment is the fear that if I eat more, the weight will start coming back.\n\nLike when my wife puts butter on something, or she gives me what she sees as a proper full plate, I can feel myself tense up before I’ve even started eating. I start thinking, “If I let this happen, that’s the beginning of it.” Not just that one meal, but the next one and the next, and then I won’t be able to stop it. It feels like I’ll lose the control I’ve worked hard to keep.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s less, “I’m trying to get to one exact body shape,” and more, “I’m frightened of putting weight back on.” That’s what makes me want to eat less.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mainly the fear of gaining weight, yes. I mean, the body shape part is mixed in with it, I won’t pretend it isn’t, but what really sets me off is the thought that the weight will start coming back.

It happens with quite ordinary things at home. If my wife puts butter on something, or if she gives me what she thinks is a proper full plate, I feel myself getting nervous almost straight away. It’s not just the butter or that one plate, it’s what I imagine it leading to. I start thinking, if I let this go, then I’ll start putting weight back on, and then I won’t be able to stop it. Like I’ll lose the control I’ve been working so hard to keep.

So I’d say it’s not so much, “I must keep this exact shape,” as it is, “I mustn’t gain.” That’s the thing that gets hold of me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is mostly my wife. She’s been after me for a while, saying I’m not eating enough, and she thought I ought to talk to someone about it. I start thinking, “Well, if I have that today, then tomorrow it’ll be more,” and before I know it I’ll be gaining weight again.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m trying to lose a lot of weight, exactly. I’m still getting around the house, still doing my chores, still managing to go on my little tours and outings when I want to. So from my point of view, if I’m still functioning and doing what I need to do, it’s hard for me to see it as being dangerously underweight or anything like that.\n\nMaybe the numbers look low to other people, I don’t know, but I don’t feel like I’m wasting away the way they seem to imply. I feel more like I’ve just adjusted to needing less.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Afraid is a strong word, but… yes, I suppose I am, in my own way. It’s not like I sit there trembling over a pound or two, but when my wife tries to put butter on my toast, or she serves me a proper full plate, I get very uneasy.\n\nI start thinking that if I let that happen, if I go back to eating like that, I’ll start putting weight back on. And then it feels like all the control I’ve managed to keep will slip away. I know she means well, and I know to her it probably just looks like a normal meal, but to me it feels like the beginning of losing hold of things.\n\nSo yes… I do get nervous about gaining weight. Maybe more than I like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose it depends what you mean by significant. My doctor does say the number on the scale has got too low, and he seemed rather concerned about it. He looks at it very differently than I do, I think.\n\nTo me, being lighter hasn’t felt entirely like a bad thing. I can get around the garden a bit more easily, and when I go for my walks I feel like I’m not carrying as much of myself, if that makes sense. I know that may sound like I’m brushing it off, and perhaps I am a little, but that’s honestly how it feels to me day to day.\n\nSo yes, the number has gone down enough that my doctor is saying it’s too low. I just don’t always feel it in the alarming way other people seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would stop restricting your food intake if you reached a weight that you consider acceptable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there is some particular number where I’d suddenly say, “Right, that’s acceptable, now I’ll eat normally again.” It doesn’t really work that neatly in my head.\n\nI suppose what brings me in, more than anything, is that my wife thinks I’m not eating enough. She watches what I have and gets upset about it. But to me, most days, coffee and a piece of toast in the morning feels perfectly adequate. Then later on I might have a bit of soup, or some vegetables, something light. I don’t feel the need for a full plate the way I used to.\n\nAt my age, big meals just feel unnecessary, almost excessive. So if I reached some weight other people considered better, I’m not sure I’d suddenly want to start eating more. I’d probably still feel that smaller meals make sense for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently lower than it should be?\n\nPatient: Lower than it should be… well, I suppose that’s where I struggle a bit, because I don’t really see it the way everyone else seems to.\n\nMy wife and the doctor look at me and act as though it’s this very serious thing, and I don’t mean to dismiss them, but I honestly don’t understand why there’s quite so much fuss about it. I’m 78, you know. People my age aren’t meant to eat like they did at 40 or 50. You slow down, you don’t need as much, your appetite isn’t the same, and carrying less weight doesn’t strike me as automatically bad.\n\nI’m still getting through my usual bits and pieces. I do my chores around the house, I keep up with things in the garden, I go out for my walks and little tours around where I like to go. If I were lying in bed all day or unable to manage, maybe I’d think differently. But as long as I’m still doing those things, it’s hard for me to feel that my weight is dangerously low or anything like that.\n\nSo… lower than some people would like, yes, clearly. Lower than it “should” be? I’m not fully convinced of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner is more attractive or desirable?\n\nPatient: Attractive… I don’t know that I’d use that word exactly, not at my age. It’s not as though I’m trying to look handsome or impress anyone, really. But desirable, perhaps, in the sense that I feel more… proper, maybe, when there’s less of me.\n\nI notice little things. If I put my belt on and it sits a notch looser, there’s a part of me that thinks, well, good, I’ve done something right. Like I’ve kept myself in hand. It feels tidy somehow, controlled. I know that may sound odd, but it gives me a sort of satisfaction.\n\nAnd then other days, if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little rounded, I feel quite put off. Sloppy is the word that comes to mind. Disappointed in myself, even if no one else would notice it. I’ll stand there and think, no, that doesn’t look right, that’s not how I want to be.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m chasing being “attractive,” exactly. But thinner does feel better to me. It feels more acceptable. More like I’m doing things properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control over your eating if you started having bigger meals again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that is what worries me, though I don’t always put it in those words.\n\nIf my wife serves me what she calls a “proper” meal, a full plate, I feel myself tense up before I’ve even started. Or if she tries to put butter on toast, or says, “You need a bit more than that,” I get nervous. It’s not just the food itself, it’s the feeling that if I give in to that, then I’ll start going back the other way — putting weight on, losing the control I’ve worked quite hard to keep.\n\nI know one meal probably doesn’t sound like much to someone else. But to me it feels like the beginning of something. Like if I start having bigger portions again, then where does it stop? I’ve kept things quite measured for myself, and when that gets disrupted, I feel unsettled. So yes, I suppose there is a fear there that I’d lose my grip on it.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to maintain your current weight or to continue losing weight?\n\nPatient: I’d say my goal is mostly to stay where I am… though if I’m honest, I do get anxious about going upward. That’s the part I notice more than any wish to keep losing and losing.\n\nWhen my wife puts a full plate in front of me, or tries to add butter to something, I feel this sort of tightness straightaway, like, no, that’s too much. It isn’t just the meal in front of me. It’s what I imagine comes after it — that I’ll start putting weight back on, and then I’ll lose the control I feel I’ve worked quite hard to keep.\n\nSo I tell myself I’m trying to maintain things, keep it steady, keep it sensible. But really, I suppose I’m also trying very hard not to gain. That’s what makes me nervous. If I eat more than I’m comfortable with, it feels like the beginning of slipping backwards.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happy with your body if you were at a healthy weight for your age and height?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I would be, no. I know “healthy weight” sounds sensible, and I don’t mean to argue with the idea of it, but when I picture it on myself, I don’t feel comfortable.\n\nIt’s the small things that seem to affect me more than they probably should. If my belt feels a notch looser, I get this quiet feeling that I’ve done right by myself somehow, like I’ve kept myself in order. It reassures me. But if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit rounded, I feel… disappointed, really. Sloppy is the word that comes to mind, though I know that may sound harsh.\n\nSo if being at a healthy weight meant seeing more of that roundness, or feeling my clothes tighter, I don’t think I’d feel happy with it. I might understand it in my head, but emotionally, no, I think I’d struggle.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to eat as little as you do now if you didn't have to worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose if gaining weight truly wasn’t a worry at all, then maybe I wouldn’t feel the need to keep it quite so tight… but it’s hard for me to imagine that, because the worry is sort of wrapped up in the eating.\n\nWhen my wife tries to put butter on things, or gives me what she thinks is a decent full plate, I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s a bit more food.” I immediately get nervous that it’s the start of putting weight back on. It feels like if I allow that, then I’m giving up the control I’ve worked quite hard to keep. So the eating less is partly because I’m afraid of what happens if I don’t.\n\nIf you could somehow take away that fear completely, then maybe I wouldn’t be so strict. But sitting here honestly, I can’t really separate the two in my mind. The thought of eating more brings the thought of weight coming back almost straight after it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still restrict your food intake if you knew you couldn't gain weight no matter what you ate?\n\nPatient: If I truly knew I couldn’t gain weight no matter what I ate… I suppose I might not feel the same need to restrict it, at least not in the same way. But it’s difficult, because in my mind food and weight are so tied together that I don’t really know how to separate them.\n\nWhen my wife puts a full plate in front of me, or if she tries to add butter to something, I feel nervous almost immediately. It’s not just, “That’s a bit rich,” or “I’m not hungry.” It’s more that I picture myself starting to put weight back on, and then I feel like I’d lose the control I’ve worked hard to keep. That thought comes so quickly that it’s hard to imagine eating freely, even if someone told me there would be no weight gain.\n\nSo… maybe in theory I wouldn’t restrict as much. But emotionally, I think I’d still be cautious, because the fear is right there before I’ve even had a chance to think it through.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern about eating more that you will gain weight, or that you will not be as thin as you want to be?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight, yes… though the two feel very close together to me. It’s not like I sit there and think in a neat way, “I want to be this thin.” It’s more that when eating more is put in front of me, I immediately worry it will lead to weight coming back.\n\nLike when my wife tries to put butter on something, or she gives me what she considers a proper full plate, I can feel myself tense up before I’ve even eaten it. I start thinking, “If I let this happen, this is how it starts,” and then I’ll be putting weight back on and losing the control I’ve tried so hard to keep. That’s the part that frightens me.\n\nSo I suppose not being as thin as I’d like is part of it, but the main thing is the weight gain — the sense that eating more means things will get away from me.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating mostly because you are afraid of what will happen if you gain weight, or is it because you want to maintain a certain body image?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear of what happens if I gain weight, I think. The body image part is there, but it isn’t as clear in my head as, “I must look exactly like this.” It’s more that if I eat more, I immediately imagine the weight coming back, and then I feel like I’ve lost control.\n\nLike I’ve said, when my wife tries to put butter on something, or she gives me a plate that she thinks is just a normal full meal, I get nervous straightaway. I can feel myself thinking, “This is how it starts,” as if one bit more will lead to another, and then suddenly I’ll be putting weight back on. It’s not really calm or logical in the moment. It feels like I’ve worked to keep things under control, and eating more threatens that.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s mostly the fear of gaining weight and losing that control, rather than just wanting to maintain a particular image.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how you think you would look or about the health problems it could cause?\n\nPatient: It’s more about how I think I would look, I suppose… or maybe not just “look,” but what it would mean if I saw the weight coming back on me. I don’t really sit there thinking about health problems from gaining weight. That’s not what usually comes into my head.\n\nWhat happens is more immediate than that. If my wife puts a full plate in front of me, or she says, “You need a bit of butter on that,” I get this nervous feeling straightaway. I start thinking that if I allow that, then I’ll start putting weight back on, and then I’ll lose the control I’ve been trying so hard to keep. It feels like one thing will lead to another.\n\nSo no, it’s not really that I’m frightened of health problems from gaining weight. It’s more the thought of my body changing and me not being able to stop it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to restrict your food intake if being thin wasn't a consideration for you?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, probably not in the same way. If being thin truly didn’t matter to me at all, I don’t know that I’d be so fixed on cutting back.\n\nBecause a lot of it is tied up in those little signs I look for, even if I don’t always say that out loud. Like if my belt feels a notch looser, there’s this feeling of, well, I’ve done right by myself today. As if that means I’ve kept myself in order. But then if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the slightest bit rounded, I feel sloppy… disappointed in myself, really. It can change how I feel about the whole day.\n\nSo when you ask if I’d still restrict if thinness wasn’t part of it, I suppose the answer is that thinness is very much part of it. Not always in some grand way, but in those small checks and feelings. The belt, the mirror, how my middle looks. Those things do affect whether I feel I should eat less.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to eat less mostly about achieving a certain body shape or mostly about avoiding weight gain?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly about avoiding weight gain, I think. The shape part is there somewhere, but the thing that really grabs me in the moment is the fear that if I eat more, the weight will start coming back.\n\nLike when my wife puts butter on something, or she gives me what she sees as a proper full plate, I can feel myself tense up before I’ve even started eating. I start thinking, “If I let this happen, that’s the beginning of it.” Not just that one meal, but the next one and the next, and then I won’t be able to stop it. It feels like I’ll lose the control I’ve worked hard to keep.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s less, “I’m trying to get to one exact body shape,” and more, “I’m frightened of putting weight back on.” That’s what makes me want to eat less.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating pattern primarily driven by a fear of gaining weight or by a desire to maintain your current body shape?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the fear of gaining weight, yes. I mean, the body shape part is mixed in with it, I won’t pretend it isn’t, but what really sets me off is the thought that the weight will start coming back.\n\nIt happens with quite ordinary things at home. If my wife puts butter on something, or if she gives me what she thinks is a proper full plate, I feel myself getting nervous almost straight away. It’s not just the butter or that one plate, it’s what I imagine it leading to. I start thinking, if I let this go, then I’ll start putting weight back on, and then I won’t be able to stop it. Like I’ll lose the control I’ve been working so hard to keep.\n\nSo I’d say it’s not so much, “I must keep this exact shape,” as it is, “I mustn’t gain.” That’s the thing that gets hold of me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is mostly my wife. She’s been after me for a while, saying I’m not eating enough, and she thought I ought to talk to someone about it. I don’t know that I’d have come in on my own, to be honest.\n\nMost days I just have coffee and a piece of toast in the morning, and then later on I might have some soup, or maybe some vegetables, something light like that. I don’t really see the need for big meals anymore. At my age, they just feel unnecessary, and sitting down to a full plate seems like more than I need. My wife worries about it, but to me it just feels… sensible, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight with this eating pattern?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way, like I’m on some sort of diet. But… yes, I suppose part of it is that I don’t want to put weight back on.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding foolish. If my wife puts butter on my toast, or she makes up a full plate for me at supper, I get this uneasy feeling, like if I start eating that way again I’ll lose the control I’ve managed to keep. I know she thinks she’s just helping, and maybe she is, but to me it feels like too much. I start thinking, “Well, if I have that today, then tomorrow it’ll be more,” and before I know it I’ll be gaining weight again.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m trying to lose a lot of weight, exactly. It’s more that I’m trying not to go back the other way. Keeping things light feels safer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: Underweight? I don’t really think of myself that way, no. You slow down, you don’t need great big meals, and it seems natural to me that your body changes a bit. I feel more like I’ve just adjusted to needing less.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Afraid is a strong word, but… yes, I suppose I am, in my own way. And then it feels like all the control I’ve managed to keep will slip away. My doctor does say the number on the scale has got too low, and he seemed rather concerned about it. I can get around the garden a bit more easily, and when I go for my walks I feel like I’m not carrying as much of myself, if that makes sense. I know that may sound like I’m brushing it off, and perhaps I am a little, but that’s honestly how it feels to me day to day.\n\nSo yes, the number has gone down enough that my doctor is saying it’s too low. I just don’t always feel it in the alarming way other people seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would stop restricting your food intake if you reached a weight that you consider acceptable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there is some particular number where I’d suddenly say, “Right, that’s acceptable, now I’ll eat normally again.” It doesn’t really work that neatly in my head.\n\nI suppose what brings me in, more than anything, is that my wife thinks I’m not eating enough. She watches what I have and gets upset about it. But to me, most days, coffee and a piece of toast in the morning feels perfectly adequate. Then later on I might have a bit of soup, or some vegetables, something light. I don’t feel the need for a full plate the way I used to.\n\nAt my age, big meals just feel unnecessary, almost excessive. So if I reached some weight other people considered better, I’m not sure I’d suddenly want to start eating more. I’d probably still feel that smaller meals make sense for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently lower than it should be?\n\nPatient: Lower than it should be… well, I suppose that’s where I struggle a bit, because I don’t really see it the way everyone else seems to.\n\nMy wife and the doctor look at me and act as though it’s this very serious thing, and I don’t mean to dismiss them, but I honestly don’t understand why there’s quite so much fuss about it. I’m 78, you know. People my age aren’t meant to eat like they did at 40 or 50. You slow down, you don’t need as much, your appetite isn’t the same, and carrying less weight doesn’t strike me as automatically bad.\n\nI’m still getting through my usual bits and pieces. I do my chores around the house, I keep up with things in the garden, I go out for my walks and little tours around where I like to go. If I were lying in bed all day or unable to manage, maybe I’d think differently. But as long as I’m still doing those things, it’s hard for me to feel that my weight is dangerously low or anything like that.\n\nSo… lower than some people would like, yes, clearly. Lower than it “should” be? I’m not fully convinced of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner is more attractive or desirable?\n\nPatient: Attractive… I don’t know that I’d use that word exactly, not at my age. It’s not as though I’m trying to look handsome or impress anyone, really. But desirable, perhaps, in the sense that I feel more… proper, maybe, when there’s less of me.\n\nI notice little things. If I put my belt on and it sits a notch looser, there’s a part of me that thinks, well, good, I’ve done something right. Like I’ve kept myself in hand. It feels tidy somehow, controlled. I know that may sound odd, but it gives me a sort of satisfaction.\n\nAnd then other days, if I catch myself in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little rounded, I feel quite put off. Sloppy is the word that comes to mind. Disappointed in myself, even if no one else would notice it. I’ll stand there and think, no, that doesn’t look right, that’s not how I want to be.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m chasing being “attractive,” exactly. But thinner does feel better to me. It feels more acceptable. More like I’m doing things properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control over your eating if you started having bigger meals again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that is what worries me, though I don’t always put it in those words.\n\nIf my wife serves me what she calls a “proper” meal, a full plate, I feel myself tense up before I’ve even started. Or if she tries to put butter on toast, or says, “You need a bit more than that,” I get nervous. It’s not just the food itself, it’s the feeling that if I give in to that, then I’ll start going back the other way — putting weight on, losing the control I’ve worked quite hard to keep.\n\nI know one meal probably doesn’t sound like much to someone else. But to me it feels like the beginning of something. Like if I start having bigger portions again, then where does it stop? I’ve kept things quite measured for myself, and when that gets disrupted, I feel unsettled. So yes, I suppose there is a fear there that I’d lose my grip on it.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to maintain your current weight or to continue losing weight?\n\nPatient: I’d say my goal is mostly to stay where I am… though if I’m honest, I do get anxious about going upward. That’s the part I notice more than any wish to keep losing and losing.\n\nWhen my wife puts a full plate in front of me, or tries to add butter to something, I feel this sort of tightness straightaway, like, no, that’s too much. It isn’t just the meal in front of me. It’s what I imagine comes after it — that I’ll start putting weight back on, and then I’ll lose the control I feel I’ve worked quite hard to keep.\n\nSo I tell myself I’m trying to maintain things, keep it steady, keep it sensible. But really, I suppose I’m also trying very hard not to gain. That’s what makes me nervous. If I eat more than I’m comfortable with, it feels like the beginning of slipping backwards.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happy with your body if you were at a healthy weight for your age and height?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I would be, no. I know “healthy weight” sounds sensible, and I don’t mean to argue with the idea of it, but when I picture it on myself, I don’t feel comfortable.\n\nIt’s the small things that seem to affect me more than they probably should. But it’s difficult, because in my mind food and weight are so tied together that I don’t really know how to separate them.\n\nWhen my wife puts a full plate in front of me, or if she tries to add butter to something, I feel nervous almost immediately. It’s not just, “That’s a bit rich,” or “I’m not hungry.” It’s more that I picture myself starting to put weight back on, and then I feel like I’d lose the control I’ve worked hard to keep. That thought comes so quickly that it’s hard to imagine eating freely, even if someone told me there would be no weight gain.\n\nSo… maybe in theory I wouldn’t restrict as much. It’s not like I sit there and think in a neat way, “I want to be this thin.” It’s more that when eating more is put in front of me, I immediately worry it will lead to weight coming back.\n\nLike when my wife tries to put butter on something, or she gives me what she considers a proper full plate, I can feel myself tense up before I’ve even eaten it. I start thinking, “If I let this happen, this is how it starts,” and then I’ll be putting weight back on and losing the control I’ve tried so hard to keep. That’s the part that frightens me.\n\nSo I suppose not being as thin as I’d like is part of it, but the main thing is the weight gain — the sense that eating more means things will get away from me.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating mostly because you are afraid of what will happen if you gain weight, or is it because you want to maintain a certain body image?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear of what happens if I gain weight, I think. The body image part is there, but it isn’t as clear in my head as, “I must look exactly like this.” It’s more that if I eat more, I immediately imagine the weight coming back, and then I feel like I’ve lost control.\n\nLike I’ve said, when my wife tries to put butter on something, or she gives me a plate that she thinks is just a normal full meal, I get nervous straightaway. It’s more about not wanting to gain weight, I’d say. Not wanting to be fat is part of the feeling, I suppose, but the thing that really frightens me is the idea that the weight will start coming back.

I start thinking, if I eat that, then that’s the start of putting weight back on. And then I worry I won’t be able to stop it, that I’ll lose the control I’ve worked so hard to keep.

I’m 78, you know. People my age aren’t meant to eat like they did at 40 or 50.
